#6843. This Brand-New Illinois Farmhouse Was Designed to Look Hundreds of Years Old

The team at Buccellato Design and Architecture focused on incorporating heritage details and timeworn furnishings.

For Aimee and Kevin Buccellato, the best compliment they could receive about a project isn’t, “Wow, it looks so different in here.” It’s that a home looks like they haven’t changed a thing.

On a country road in Momence, Illinois, a beloved farmhouse stands as a timeworn landmark for the surrounding agricultural community. For the homeowners—a retired couple returning to their Midwestern roots—the property was as much a repository of memories as it was a place to create new ones. “Having grown up nearby, the property evoked a strong sense of memory and place,” says Aimee, who founded Buccellato Design and Architecture alongside her husband, Kevin. “At the outset of the project, and throughout, our clients wanted the new house to feel like it had always been there, so much so that perhaps the neighbors could be led to believe that the original house remained intact, yet amended by skillful renovation and discreet additions.”

It was an architectural sleight of hand rooted in a distinctly Midwestern philosophy: “Be good to the neighbors.” Alongside the Buccellatos, the couple approached the 6,000-square-foot rebuild with the restraint of preservationists and the imagination of storytellers. “Great care was taken to instill the home and outbuildings with a sense of history and rootedness, creating a home that feels as though it’s been part of this bucolic setting for centuries,” says Kevin of the project, which also included the restoration of the property’s original 19th-century barn and corn crib. “We drew inspiration from traditional farm complexes, where homesteads were added slowly over time, reconnecting the site with its Folk-Victorian-Farm-House and agricultural building roots.”

Inside, the narrative continues through antique white oak floors, salvaged beams, heritage florals, and rooms that borrow their palettes from the landscape. In the kitchen, a wall of windows replaces upper cabinetry, framing sunrise over the barn and walnut trees. The glass porch offers sweeping views of the Kankakee River, while a tucked-away speakeasy recalls the original farmhouse’s stone foundation.

For Kevin and Aimee, the guiding principle was a new construction home that felt “sensitive, not wasteful, polite, not ostentatious,” an exercise in knowing when architecture and interiors should defer to their surroundings. The result is a house with an invented past and a very real sense of belonging that does the Midwest—and the neighbors—proud.

FAST FACTSDesigner: Buccellato Design and ArchitectureStylist: Cate RaganLocation: Momence, IllinoisThe Space: A four-bedroom, four-and-a-half-bathroom home across 6,000 square feet

The entryway offers a fitting introduction to the home'’s carefully constructed sense of history, replete with nickel gap paneling, fumed and waxed antique white oak floors, and a light-filled staircase. Even the furnishings—including a vintage Persian rug and antique Jacobean gateleg table—suggest a collection acquired over generations.

Benjamin Moore’s Nelson Blue sets a serene backdrop for the sitting room, where traditional silhouettes and understated patterns make an easy pairing. “This room was designed to honor the history of this property and embody the love that our clients hold for this special place,” adds Aimee.

A tufted mohair sofa, realized in a rich umber, lends a sense of history, while an iconic feather lamp by Bunny Williams offers a reminder that even the most classic rooms benefit from a little irreverence.

In the kitchen, the Buccellatos traded upper cabinets for an expansive wall of windows overlooking the farmland, relocating the practical necessities to an adjacent dish room and larder. Salvaged white oak beams and barnboards overhead reinforce the home’s agricultural roots, while furniture-like cabinetry and leathered black quartzite countertops strike a timeless note.

With natural light pouring in from three sides, the dining room is what the Buccellatos describe as “peaceful, soft, and ethereal,” equally suited to both morning coffee and candlelit dinners. A dreamy mural wallpaper by Susan Harter references the property’s location along the Kankakee River, while formal seating lends a sense of occasion without sacrificing the home’s easygoing country charm.

Nestled among mature trees, the primary bedroom feels almost suspended in the surrounding landscape. The Buccellatos leaned into that sense of serenity with the palette. “It’s a dreamlike mix of earthy neutrals and pale blues that paints a serene backdrop for elegant details, vintage furnishings, and a delicate mix of subtle pattern,” adds Kevin.

A French country canopy bed, dressed in tailored Schumacher linen, takes center stage, while vintage-inspired furnishings and subtle pattern keep the room from feeling overly precious. An adjacent sitting room offers a place to read the morning paper or unwind with a cup of tea in the evenings.

In the vestibule, a little pattern goes a long way. A climbing floral from Schumacher in inky charcoal makes a charming counterpoint to the bedroom’s hushed palette, while a checked window bench is the ideal spot to read, giving the passageway purpose.

Pale blue walls and creamy millwork lend the primary bathroom an old-fashioned prettiness. A custom vanity introduces a tailored architectural element, while a Cowtan & Tout floral Roman shade softens the room’s more utilitarian details.

Designed as a playful respite for guests and future grandchildren, this bedroom plays off pattern and primary hues. “The red painted woodwork feels unexpected and cheerful against the blue-and-white wallpaper, striped rug, and patterned bedding,” says Aimee.

“The brick-clad main block echoes the footprint of the original farmhouse on this site, yet the story we wrote for the house begins with the folk Victorian wing, inspired by L-shaped farmhouses of that period,” says Kevin. “In our modern narrative, the clapboard-sided wing came first, followed by the brick-clad ’addition,’ which takes inspiration from the later Colonial Revival period in its scale and sophistication of detailing.”

Founded by Aimee and Kevin Buccellato, Buccellato Design & Architecture is a practice marked by twenty-plus years of close collaboration, in architectural practice and in life. With projects on both coasts and throughout the country, the firm’s award-winning work reveals a dedication to craft, artistry, and detail. Achieved in partnership with clients, their projects reflect a purposeful sense of rootedness in the landscape and abiding devotion to beauty and the comfort of home.

#6842. Queen Letizia Rewears a Favorite One-Shoulder Dress From the 2010s

The queen of Spain makes outfit repeating look so good. Today, Queen Letizia attended the 30th Ibero-American Summit 2026 held at the Royal Collections Gallery in Madrid, Spain, and for the occasion, she brought out a favorite dress of hers: a one-shoulder midi from Madrid-based brand Delpozo.

Letizia, who regularly supports the Spanish heritage label, has pulled the dress out several times before, including to the 50th anniversary celebration of Spanish sports newspaper AS in 2017, on a trip to Paris in 2018, and for the Princesa de Asturias Awards in 2020, at the peak of the COVID-19 pandemic.

She styled the midi with a different pointy pump, clutch, and elegant updo every time before, but today, she changed it up. Letizia let her hair down in glamorous waves, and instead of slipping into classic heels, she went for a pair of black patent-leather slingbacks with kitten heels.

For some extra pizzazz, she accessorized with her aquamarine-and-diamond drop earrings. The special pieces were commissioned by her husband, King Felipe VI (then Prince Felipe), from Joyería Aldao, and gifted to Letizia on the day of the birth of the couple’s second child, Infanta Sofía.

The queen paired her earrings with her striking diamond-and-sapphire bracelet—the one which was gifted to her by her in-laws, former King Juan Carlos I and Queen Sofia, upon her engagement to Felipe in 2003.

#6840. We Found the Ultimate Cold-Weather Cologne

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Fulton & Roark's new scent, inspired by a church in Gary, Indiana, is phenomenal.

Welcome to the Esquire Endorsement. Heavily researched. Thoroughly vetted. These picks are the best way to spend your hard-earned cash.

Halloween is in the rearview, Daylight Saving Time is over, and the sun has decided it has better places to be (although I am grateful for that extra hour of sleep we got). You know what all that means: It’s officially time to bring out the poofy coats, the cable-knit sweaters, and the winter scents. We’re talking spicy, smoky, sophisticated, and a little dark. Think of it as the olfactory equivalent of a cashmere scarf or quilted jacket. But before you reach for the same old colognes of winters past, consider making room for something new in your fragrance rotation.

Allow me to suggest my new cold-weather go-to, released just last month by one of my all-time favorite niche fragrance houses, Fulton & Roark. This extrait has a pretty sick name: Cathedral of Ruins—and it smells every bit as atmospheric as you’d hope for with a name like that. It’s moody without being gloomy, warm without being cloying, and oh-so intriguing. Put a spritz of this stuff on and you’re guaranteed to start hearing the words, What are you wearing?

Fulton & Roark does what it calls “American fine fragrance,” meaning it doesn’t follow all the rules of your classic European perfumery. The perfumer looks to new ingredients, formulation methods, and scent combinations for all of its fragrances. Oh, and every fragrance is unisex, truly unisex. My boyfriend loves the scents as much as I do, and we often share them.

And since this is American fine fragrance we’re talking about, it only makes sense that each of the brand’s fragrances are named after and inspired by a real American place with a story behind it. That rule even holds for the brand’s name: Fulton is for Fulton County, Georgia; and Roark is for Roark’s Cove in Sewanee, Tennessee. So where is this Cathedral of Ruins? Gary, Indiana. Birthplace of the Jackson 5, and our clearest example of Rust Belt deindustrialization. The once-booming steel city is now also home to thousands of abandoned and decaying buildings, including the City Methodist Church—once one of the grandest churches in the Midwest.

Fulton and Roark co-founder Kevin Keller showed me photos of what City Methodist Church looks like now, which he took on his own trip to Gary, and the immediate sense I got from these photos was a devastating sort of beauty (or a beautiful sort of devastation). The church’s roof has fallen in; the only living congregation is the vegetation that reclaims more and more of the space every year. But, its Neo-Gothic stone walls still stand. It’s a dead relic, but it is not a dead space. It pulls up mixed feelings. This Cathedral of Ruins extrait de parfum is Fulton & Roark’s homage to it, and it nails everything you get from seeing a picture of the place.

Like I said, Cathedral of Ruins is the perfect cold-weather scent. But instead of the typical rich, gourmand notes you get in fall and winter fragrances like vanilla, caramel, and amber, Cathedral of Ruins relies on spices, smoke, and woods to create a warm atmosphere. This is not a syrupy sweet winter scent—it’s smoldering, Gothic-inspired, sophisticated, and unlike any scent you’ve ever smelled before.

At the top, you get resinous cypress, spicy black peppercorn, and, unexpectedly, floral rose. The lush, velvety notes of rose lead into the more delicate floral notes of saffron, symbolizing the grace of the cathedral (and possibly the new natural growth of vegetation over the ruins). Rich Peru balsam finishes out the heart with more warm, woody vibes.

But the base is where this fragrance really stands out. The star of the show here is burned frankincense, the classic scent of sacred ritual—imagine smoke rising above the walls, past the ceiling that’s no longer there. Frankincense is, obviously, smoky, but not in the same way as something like tobacco; the frankincense here is dry, resinous, and subtly fresh like pine. To me, frankincense even has a waxy-green quality similar to olives. The warm depth of leathered woods and musks finish out the fragrance, leaving the intrigue of smoky woods to linger on your skin.

#6838. 4 Weekend Decluttering Habits That Make Small Spaces Feel Bigger

A few smart systems can keep your counters, closets, and junk drawers in check.

The easiest clutter to manage is clutter that never has a chance to form. And in small spaces like galley kitchens and studio apartments, mess piles up naturally on any flat surface. Keeping it under control is less about “magic bullet” tips and more about systems you can actually keep up over time. So when we asked the professional organizers about how to declutter small spaces, the answer was in mindset and habits. If you can limit what comes into your space in the first place, you can better control its organization.

“I could say, ‘Grab a garbage bag and pick up all of the detritus left in the wake of a busy life and then put everything else where it belongs.’ That’s obvious,” Gayle M. Gruenberg of Let’s Get Organized says. “The hard part is actually DOING it. It requires motivation, presence of mind, initiative, and the necessary tools.”

Below, find the pro-approved habits that make a difference when you have more stuff than your space can hold. You can start these routines in just one weekend, and it’s far more attainable than tackling the massive pile in the garage.

Everything needs a home in your house. That’s the power of a well-placed “drop zone.” Even in small spaces, a catchall tray, bowl, or mini basket belongs at entryways or places of transition. A hook for your keys and bags when you come through the door, a bin for loose throw blankets, or a basket for remote controls on the mantel.

Throughout the day, it can take quite a bit of mental load to always put everything back when you use it. That’s where Cathy Orr, cofounder of The Uncluttered Life, recommends having a “collection basket.” It’s just one catchall container, where you can drop miscellaneous misplaced items that you pick up throughout the day. At the end of the night, take about 10 minutes to go through the collection basket and return everything to where it belongs. It’s just one small habit, rather than something to keep up with all day.

Impulse shopping lends itself to clutter very easily. The “One In, One Out” rule can curb this. It works like this: for every new thing you want to bring into your home, one thing has to go. Greenhalgh suggests checking your closet, fridge, and pantry before you shop—going through your back stock will save you both space and money.

“In small spaces, items accumulate very quickly,” Macie Kreutzer, founder of For the Love of Simple, says. “Before shopping for new items (home goods, clothing) clear out at least one item from this category. Don't bring in more than your space can physically contain.”

Once you have everything you’re ready to get rid of, make the donation process as seamless as possible. Fill your donation bags and boxes in the hallway or in the car. This encourages you to actually finish getting rid of the clutter, rather than letting them collect dust in the garage.

“Nothing gets to wander back inside just because it once lived there,” Carroll Cartwright, professional organizer and owner of Neatly & Co., says. “And this is why I love moving everything out of the space and into the hall or a separate area first. The things you decide not to keep are already halfway out the door. Keep them moving.”

It is so tempting to take advantage of the freebies in life. But this is how linen closets and kitchen drawers get quickly overrun. Unless there’s a specific and immediate need for them, you’re better off getting rid of them.

“They are usually cheap and break easily anyway,” Diane Greenhalgh, owner of Tiny to the Max, says. “That includes airplane and hotel freebies, special event or conference swag, and extra takeout plastic utensils and condiments. Your junk drawer will thank you.”

If you already have a drawer overflowing with freebies, it’s time to start culling and evaluating. You might part with two or 10 things, or you might choose to keep it all. “The point is that you’ve looked at every single item and made a decision about it, instead of continuing to store things simply because they were already there,” Cartwright says. “More likely than not, you’ll find something that makes you say, ‘Why on earth do I still have this?’”

The advice your grandma gave you was spot-on: It’s easier if you wash the dishes as you cook. That same principle applies to keeping your small space tidy. Mess feels larger with less square footage to work with, so once you’re finished with something, put it away.

“Put away your makeup after you've applied it in the morning, stack dishes in the dishwasher and wipe down surfaces after each meal, and tuck away work or homework into a drawer once you're done for the evening,” Greenhalgh says.

For the days you can’t clean as you go, Cartwright suggests spending 10 to 15 minutes a day “closing your circles.” It’s what helps your home stay clean over time, instead of just for a few weeks. This period is when you put the scissors back in the drawer or hang up your jacket.

“Most clutter is just a collection of unfinished tasks,” Cartwright says. “This small commitment allows you to wake up to order instead of yesterday’s chaos, and that is a much better way to start the day!”

#6837. After the Midterms, Millennial Optimism Makes Sense Again

None of us are the same people we were in 2008. But it’s okay, just for a day, to bask in hope.

I have told this story many times in writing, but in 2008 I was so hopeful on election night that I stripped off my clothes with a bar full of people, right before they called Pennsylvania for Obama, to take a picture and commemorate the moment. They took the photo. We got up off the sticky bar floor, pulled our clothes back on, and literally danced in the streets with the cops while the Obama reggaeton song played. This was peak millennial political optimism, and the rush of it could make you believe that all the contradictions of this country didn’t matter. That we were all moving forward together.

In 2008, I was a junior researcher at a Black history museum, a place where people thought deeply about legacy and historical sea change. I remember being so deeply excited, after seven years of political insanity post-9/11, that maybe the United States was finally waking up. It was a shock, then, to talk to my older coworkers, most of whom had been involved with or studied the Black radical tradition, and hear their cautious indifference to what felt like a coming revolution. “We’ll see,” most of them said—a pragmatism about this country that I didn’t fully understand back then. Back then I hoped, deep down, that what those older coworkers were experiencing was merely sour grapes.

In 2026, of course, we are all living through the political backlash that they were sure was coming and the missteps of moderation during the Obama years that set the stage for Trump. I don’t really hope about politics anymore. Yes, I know hope is a discipline. Hope is something that, by definition, you have to have when things are dire. But the way hope lives in my body—a frantic, desperate thing that plummets when it meets catastrophe—means national politics aren’t a good place for that emotion. When I talk about politics now with friends, it’s mostly to see who can make the darkest joke about our current timeline.

But after last night, things are different. Like a lot of people, I’m allowing myself one calendar day to actually feel excited about the state of the world. I’m still saying “Let’s see,” but for right now, every time someone tries to bring up why we’re still doomed, I keep scrolling. I can do bad all by myself tomorrow.

Since Trump entered the scene 10 years ago, there’s been an active disdain for sincerity in politics. The reasons for this are many. Trump and MAGA ran on the belief that becoming great could only be measured by how badly the people you hated were suffering. Those who would oppose Trump grew cynical about the national Democratic party’s dance of fearmongering about the state of the country and steadfastly refusing to do anything meaningful about it when given the power to do so. Between these two extremes, it felt foolish to care. It felt smarter and safer to view politics the way a consultant or a pundit might, with the distance of someone who keeps telling you he has no skin in the game. As if that position was a superior one. As if that distance could save you any heartbreak or keep the threat of a layoff or a medical bill or an eviction at bay.

To care is to be cringe, and in the last few years, being cringe has been one of the greatest cultural sins, a devastating social faux pas. But last night was such a seismic shift in American politics that the idea of cringe is currently blissfully irrelevant. Being cringe enough to care brought millions to the polls and a historic midterm turnout. Being cringe enough to care meant that people like James Talarico, Abdul El-Sayed, and Angie Nixon launched campaigns even as everyone told their potential base that voting was irrelevant, that nothing could ever change about their states.

I’m not the same person I was in 2008; hopefully none of us are. And this is not the same country. But to make sure this country is one where I want to be present, I have to believe on some level that it’s possible. To change anything in this world, you first have to care enough to want to do it.

#6836. Asking for a Friend, Is the Silent Treatment Really That Bad?

Therapists explain why going quiet during conflict can be so damaging.

In a perfect world, we’d all be emotionally mature, communicatively adept lovergirls (gender-neutral, BTW) dating the same. But to the chagrin of the verbal processors and chatterboxes amongst us, some of us wind up with someone who goes silent in the face of strife. If you’ve ever been on the receiving end of the silent treatment and spiraled, this article is for you.

First, quick definition: “The silent treatment is unexplained, open-ended, and intentional withdrawal of connection and communication to control or manipulate the other partner,” Stacy Hubbard, LMFT, an EMDR-certified trauma therapist and certified Gottman therapist and Gottman master trainer based in Ashland, Oregon, tells Cosmopolitan.

Sure, sometimes this looks like blatant ignoring of your questions and bids for connection. But it can also mean withholding positive nonverbal responses (think: smiles, eye contact, affectionate touch) or swapping them with gestures that ooze contempt, like scoffing or eye-rolling, says Kimberly Panganiban, LMFT, a certified Gottman couples therapist based in San Diego, California. And because we live in the age of the smartphone, “it can also mean not responding to text messages,” she adds.

But now for what you’re really wondering: Why the heck does my partner go hush, and is it as bad as it feels? Ahead, relationship therapists explain what's behind the radio silence, what it means for your relationship, and how to get the chit-chat going again.

Bluntly, the silent treatment is neither a healthy nor an effective way to deal with conflict and other hard-to-swallow feelings like disappointment, anger, or hurt. “The silent treatment is concerning anytime—but it becomes especially concerning if it is used regularly,” says Panganiban.

“Being on the receiving end of the silent treatment can be very distressing for the partner on the receiving end of it,” says Panganiban. If you’ve ever begged for so much as a syllable—let alone a full-ass sentence—from someone dead-set on giving you the cold shoulder, you know how true that is.

Suddenly, the person who is supposed to be your confidant and cheerleader, first call and best friend, is as likely to answer your texts or calls as your celebrity crush is to slide into your DMs Adam Levine style with a “Holy fck. Holy fucking fck. That body of yours is absurd.” (IYKYK). With that comes an avalanche of doubt and despair, burying you in questions about where you stand and whether your relationship will weather the storm.

Through an attachment theory lens, the person who should be your secure base suddenly becomes a source of uncertainty. Over time, this can create a sense that your beloved attention, love, and care are conditional, which “will start to impact the level of trust in the relationship,” Panganiban says.

“Trust requires that both partners feel that their partner has their back at all times (even in conflict),” she explains. When one partner withdraws instead of working through the issue, the receiving partner may begin to question if their partner has their best interests at heart.

To top it off, Panganiban says the silent treatment robs a relationship of the chance to reap the bounty of healthy conflict management, including mutual understanding, emotional intimacy, and the chance to come back together even stronger.

The silent treatment works about as well as a rubber with holes in it… so why do people do it? In part, because many of us never learned how to actually resolve conflict.

Few of us took a How to Fight Better class in high school. Instead, we got our so-called “lessons” in conflict resolution from loved ones who relied on ineffective (read: downright harmful) methods to express hurt, get their point across, or meet their needs. Indeed, many adults who give the silent treatment witnessed their parents give it when they were kids, or received it themselves, according to Hubbard.

“When a child is frequently given the silent treatment, they may mimic the behavior of the parent and become the silent partner growing up,” says licensed clinical professional counselor Kristine Cain, LCPC, founder and lead therapist of Full Circle Counseling in Frankfort, Illinois.

Conversely, some children learn that the only way to restore connection is to set their own needs aside and focus all their energy on appeasing the withdrawn parent. As adults, this tendency to prioritize someone else's emotional needs over their own may leave them particularly vulnerable to relationships where they repeatedly receive the silent treatment, she explains.

Not all silence is a calculated, conniving attempt to control your partner like a marionette. Sometimes, it's a sign that someone's nervous system has gone into overdrive.

In a society that teaches us to prioritize production over pleasure, pushing through over slowing down, we aren't exactly taught to tune into our bodies… despite living in them 24/7. As a result, people aren't always aware of the physiological symptoms that signal a need to step away from an argument, Panganiban says.

She explains: When you're fighting with someone you love, your body doesn't necessarily register the disagreement as just another Tuesday-night tiff. “Our partner is the most important person in our life, so when we are in conflict with them, it feels like a threat to our safety and our bodies respond as such,” she says.

When your nervous system interprets a disagreement with a partner as a capital-T threat, “a series of physiological changes begin to occur in your body,” says Panganiban. “Your heart rate speeds up, your body secretes adrenaline and cortisol, and blood flow to the kidneys, gut, and certain parts of the brain shuts off.” (This is known as flooding).

These changes can make it harder to use healthy communication skills, like thinking clearly, listening openly, and responding empathetically. At this point, some people will respond by fighting Jersey Shore season one style, but others will go quiet…

When someone goes quiet during a conflict because their nervous system has gone haywire, it can look a lot like the silent treatment, but it might actually be stonewalling. Popularized by relationship researchers at the Gottman Institute, stonewalling is marked by shutting down and becoming unresponsive during conflict, often because they're physiologically overwhelmed (a.k.a. flooded), says Hubbard.

“Outwardly, stonewalling and the silent treatment may look the same,” she says. But the difference lies in intent. “Stonewalling occurs when someone enters into a state of diffuse physiological arousal and withdraws as an attempt to de-escalate and self-regulate,” says Panganiban. By contrast, the silent treatment may be a deliberate attempt to punish a partner, avoid accountability, or sidestep a difficult conversation, she explains.

That doesn’t mean that stonewalling gets a free pass. “Stonewalling is harmful,” says Hubbard. In fact, it's one of the Four Horsemen, the conflict patterns Gottman researchers identified as predictors of relationship breakdown. (ICYWW: The other three are contempt, criticism, and defensiveness).

Someone giving you the silent treatment may be trying to make you uncomfortable, while someone stonewalling is trying to make themselves comfortable. Whether your partner has gone mum because they're giving you the silent treatment or because they've become so overwhelmed that they're stonewalling you, neither is good news for your relationship.

Still, you don't need to know exactly why your partner is shutting you out to know that their silence is hurting you—and that you want to take steps to help stop it.

If you want to go the distance, Cain says it's worth being brave and naming how their withdrawal affects you.

She offers this formula: “When you go silent, I feel ________ (worthless, abandoned, not good enough, etc.). My _________ (mom, dad, friend, ex, etc.) used to do this and it is very triggering for me. Can we figure out a way to communicate better so that we don't get to that point again?”

Of course, you don't need a history of being given the silent treatment—or to unpack your childhood woes—to justify wanting your partner not to go AWOL on you. So, you could also keep it simple: “When you stop talking to me without explaining why, I feel shut out. I’d like us to work together to figure out a different way to handle those moments.”

The goal isn’t to say The Perfect Thing, but instead to make it clear that their silence isn’t working for you.

While you can’t know someone’s intentions just by tallying their actions, doing so can be a helpful thought exercise that helps you figure out whether or not this is a relationship you actually want long(er) term.

If your partner tends to shrink when a scuttles gets saucy, or has described feeling overwhelmed they may be familiar with flooding. On the other hand, if their silence kicks in any time you set a boundary, express a need, or do something they don't like, it may be their attempt at control.

Hubbard also suggests taking a beat to consider how your partner treats you most of the time. Does your partner regularly dismiss you or make you feel responsible for managing their moods? If so, their silence could be part of a larger pattern of controlling behavior. Conversely, if they tend to shut down during other emotionally intense situations—not just disagreements with you—that could suggest they struggle with flooding more broadly.

So you talk to your partner, and they’re receptive as hell? Hell yeah, brother!

If you and your partner—perhaps with the help of a relationship therapist, wink, wink—determine that physiological overwhelm is part of the picture, Panganiban recommends that you both get familiar with your personal warning signs.

“Recognizing flooding can be tricky, but everyone has different physiological indicators, so it is important that both partners learn what those are,” she says. For some, the giveaway is dry mouth; for others, it's muscle tension; and for many, it's a racing heart. Once you both know the signs, you can make a plan for what to do when they show up, she says.

Technically speaking, a flooding ritual is a strategy for managing flooding before it leads to stonewalling. But even if your partner's silence isn't the result of a nervous system gone haywire, the underlying principle of communicating when they need space and when you'll come back together can keep silence from jet-setting you to Spiral City.

Hubbard suggests picking a single word—essentially, a fighting safeword—that either of you can say to communicate that you need to take a pause, but will reconnect in 20 or 30 minutes. It could also be a nonverbal cue, such as a time-out hand motion, that communicates the same, says Panganiban.

During the pause, the goal is to reduce physiological arousal by doing anything that soothes you, says Panganiban. “It could be deep breathing, going on a walk, engaging in a hobby, watching TV, or anything else that gets your mind off of the conflict and brings your body back to baseline,” she says.

Panganiban says the most important part is returning to the conversation at the agreed-upon time, which she recommends be no more than 24 hours later. As Hubbard explains, “embedded into this ritual is the expectation that both partners will return to the conversation rather than leaving one person waiting indefinitely.”

Of course, even the world's most thoughtful flooding ritual can’t do diddly squat if your partner refuses to participate. And if they keep pulling the silent treatment out of their back pocket even after you’ve told them how much it hurts? Well, perhaps that says it all…

#6835. The Democratic Party Has Rediscovered the Power of Christianity. The GOP Should Be Worried.

James Talarico of Texas and Adam Hamilton of Kansas have proven that faith isn’t just a Republican value.

Listen to James Talarico, celebrating his massive victory over Ken Paxton, who must have a really ugly portrait of himself up in the attic.

On Tuesday night, the voters elected two Democratic preachers to Senate seats that once seemed vastly out of reach: Talarico and Adam Hamilton in Kansas. They will be joining Senator Raphael Warnock, the pastor at Martin Luther King Jr.’s church in Atlanta, in the upper chamber of the national legislature. It is possible that, after decades in the wilderness, the Democratic party may be rediscovering the true political power of the actual gospel, not the gospel preached by the megachurch charlatans making culture-war hay out of things Jesus never actually said or believed. When Talarico said that God was “nonbinary” and the performance Christians of the Right jumped all over him, he was right and they were wrong. For those who believe in him, God is pure spirit, beyond all gender. So, technically, God is nonbinary. But, anyway, Jesus never said fck all about transgender people.

For his part, Adam Hamilton is the founder of a huge Methodist church in the Kansas City area. He has given sermons in which he asked his congregation to repent of eating beef and driving cars as part of a sermon on climate change. The Republicans attempted to freakify him with these ideas, but Hamilton’s fundamental decency, and his sincere belief in “stewardship” of the planet, which used to be the Republican dodge on environmental issues, were able to overcome the usual campaign slanging. His acceptance speech called for a gentler, more charitable politics. Again, a more Christian Christianity than the one purportedly practiced by the ratfcking Pharisees of the American Right.

This is a very interesting development if it survives this election cycle and if it isn’t dependent on uniquely charismatic candidates like Talarico. If the Democrats can reckon with a lost source of political power, then that cannot be a bad development.

#6833. Irish Designers Share Their 7 Coziest Style Secrets That We Should All Be Stealing

Here’s how to embrace that “Green Wave” with your interiors.

Over the past few years, Americans have become notably charmed by Irish culture—even the L.A. Times coined it a “Green Wave.” From splitting the “G” when drinking a pint of Guinness and bragging about it on social media to spotlighting Irish celebrities like author Sally Rooney, musician Hozier, and actress Saoirse Ronan, we as a society—myself very much included—have become a bit obsessed with the Emerald Isle, and I think it’s high time we let this Green Wave roll into the interior design scene.

If you’ve ever had the privilege to visit a home in the Emerald Isle, you’ll know that Irish spaces are warm, welcoming, and lived-in, with many of the design elements specifically used to prioritize comfort and community, something American homeowners have begun adopting. “Irish homes have traditionally needed to be comfortable, functional, and adaptable to a climate that can be cold, damp, and unpredictable,” Galway-based designer Niamh McKenna tells House Beautiful. “Traditionally, our homes are also much smaller, so beautiful rooms must also work for everyday life.”

However, it’s not just the environment which influences their interiors. Keep reading to find out the quintessentially Irish design details that local designers love to celebrate and believe American homes should borrow.

During the Georgian era, which spanned from the early 1700s to the early 1800s, Ireland experienced a population boom. During this time, Dublin also underwent a sizable redevelopment when it comes to architecture and urban infrastructure, according to History Ireland. It’s these elegant details that designer Julianne Kelly loves so much and thinks others should adopt.

“There is so much history in these houses and the proportions are beautiful: high ceilings, ornate cornicing, original sash windows, fireplaces, and rich paneled doors,” Kelly says. “These details give a room a sense of history and permanence.”

The Merrion Square area in Dublin, she adds, is a great place to walk around to view some of these rich elements in person, specifically the iconic painted Georgian doors.

Once you leave the streets of Georgian Dublin, you’ll find the sprawling countryside with “a beautiful, varied landscape that is impossible to ignore when designing the interiors for a house,” Kelly explains. She uses many natural elements found just outside the window in her designs, like greenery, stone, timber, and natural fibers like wool and linen that feel authentic to the country and only improve with age. Colors inspired by the scenes outside help the interior feel connected to the earth as well.

The textiles specifically add much-needed warmth and coziness to a room. “I love these as they add personality, a sense of calm, and an old worldliness that doesn’t look over manufactured or brand-spanking new!”

“The fireplace has traditionally been at the heart of the Irish home, and I think that idea is still incredibly relevant,” Kelly says. Ornate or simple, stone or tiled, wood-burning or gas—the details of the fireplace don’t really matter, as long as it serves as a natural focal point and the furniture is arranged around it.

McKenna adds that centering the hearth rather than the TV promotes conversation and connection. “It’s a reminder that a room can be designed around how people gather and interact,” she explains. The energy and warmth of a fireplace, even as modern homes move away from open fires, brings comfort and beauty to any room, and that’s something worth preserving.

Step into an Irish person’s home, and you’ll likely find a lot of old mixed in with the new. “Irish people love to hold onto their items,” Kelly says. “I’m not sure if it’s due to our famine history, but on most projects I work on, people have sentimental items that I have to work with.”

The designer will always try to work with these existing pieces to come up with some sort of eclectic mix of modern and vintage. She loves how unique and personal these homes turn out to be; they tell a great story. “And the Irish love a good story,” she adds.

In Ireland’s rainy climate, a home’s interiors—especially practical, hardworking spaces—need to feel both beautiful and comfortable.

“Bathrooms and kitchens are among the hardest-working parts of our homes, and we see it important to give the most functional part of our homes the same thought and attention as our living spaces,” McKenna explains. “We love to connect the indoors to the outdoors in these spaces, by using natural materials, earthy colors, and organic textures to create an interior that is as beautiful to experience as it is practical to use.”

Much of the residential architecture in Ireland is designed to work with the chilly, wet climate. “Traditional Irish architecture features deep window reveals, thick walls, and smaller windows than what might be seen elsewhere,” McKenna explains.

These choices could easily make a home feel dark and cold, but the designer says that when oriented correctly to the outside light, these elements only add to a cozy, warm environment in the winter and a cool place for relief in the summer. “I love that these characteristics also create a depth, shadow, and texture that are difficult to replicate with decorative finishes alone,” she adds.

Similarly to the architecture, the interiors are decorated to account for the damp climate, as well. “We make comfort a priority rather than a finishing touch, by layering furnishings such as window treatments, tactile rugs and cushions, and blankets,” McKenna says. Even if someone were to decorate their home rather minimally, the layers of tactile fabrics found in Irish homes still ensure the space feels inviting and lived-in.

#6832. All the Exciting Ballot Wins You May Have Missed From Election Day

The 2026 midterms featured a number of key issues on states’ ballots

The midterm general elections held this Tuesday marked a major shift for the United States. A historic “blue wave” reflected the country’s overwhelming support for the Democratic Party, which now has control of both the House of Representatives and the Senate. And a number of Democratic candidates won their elections for governor too, in swing (and even non-swing) states like Texas, Georgia, Iowa, and Ohio.

Many of the surprise wins also brought about historic firsts for the nation. Keisha Lance Bottoms has become the first Black woman ever elected to governor, while Chris Pappas is the first openly gay candidate elected to the Senate. Other notable firsts include the first-ever Muslim Senate-elect (Abdul El-Sayed) and the first-ever Native American female governor to be voted in to the post (Deb Haaland).

But with so much excitement around the candidates, it can be easy to forget that yesterday also saw a number of important measures featured on ballots across all 50 states. And a few of the results were quite uplifting.

Virginia saw a complete victory for voters on the left, with citizens of the state voting to codify marriage equality, even in the case that the Supreme Court ever decides to overturn Obergefell v. Hodges; and Loving v. Virginia, which protects interracial marriage, was also codified. Virginia voters also chose to protect reproductive rights, and to restore voting rights to those who have been previously incarcerated.

Nevada citizens voted to protect abortion rights too, while those in Washington voted not to repeal a millionaire wealth tax. (The key issues that many of the ballots centered around this year included abortion rights, taxes, drug policies, voting, and transgender rights.)

There are still some ballot measures that have yet to be called, and votes will continue to roll in throughout the next few hours, meaning a few major issues remain hanging in the balance.

#6831. Erika Felt the “Math Wasn’t Really Mathing” With Blake’s Finances During ‘Love Is Blind’

There was a lot more to their breakup than we saw.

Erika and Blake were one of the many (many!) couples to end Love Is Blind Season 11 without getting married, and apparently there was more to their split than we saw on screen.

The show’s finale gave viewers a brief glimpse at Erika and Blake ending their relationship seemingly due to logistical issues: he wanted to live on a boat and sail around the world, while she wanted to live in a house and start a family. But Erika says there were some other red flags.

“I was like, ‘There’s no way this guy’s broke.’ He’s got a career and not a lot of bills from what I heard. So the math wasn’t really mathing,” she told Tudum. “Those are questions that maybe I was too afraid to ask at the beginning. They’re very sensitive and personal. But I remember him being so cool about [finances] that it almost felt like he did have money in the moment. He wasn’t sweating about it at all.”

She added that “Finances are something that, over time, you start to see some patterns and traits. Like, ‘What credit cards are they using? Are they paying with cash?’”

On top of that, Erika felt like she hadn’t really gotten to experience Blake’s world and didn’t know what she was jumping into. She literally thought to herself, “‘I can’t marry you, I haven’t seen enough of your life,’” adding, “It just started to feel really dangerous to get any closer to the wedding having already been through a divorce.”

#6828. 5 Common Mistakes That Are Making Your Vintage Decor Look All Wrong—and How Designers Get It Right

Experts say the best rooms mix eras, styles, and stories.

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Pictured above: This space, designed by Sarah West, strikes the right balance of vintage and contemporary decor, proving that a mix is always better than sticking to one design era.

It’s not just the Instagram algorithm playing tricks on you: Everyone really is looking to antique and vintage decor. Homeowners are growing tired of rooms that look as though everything was bought from a single brand in one fell swoop. We’re now at a point where a collected, curated, personalized feel has replaced quiet luxury.

“I think it’s important to add at least some kind of antique or vintage piece in every room,” says Sarah West, founder of Sarah West & Associates and a designer for over 20 years. “Even if you’re doing a more modern or transitional room, a sense of history adds weight to a space.”

It’s not necessarily as easy as plopping one antique into a room and calling it a day, however, and that can feel intimidating. Even once you learn to shop for antiques and vintage like a pro, what do you actually do with those finds when you’re home?

While the designers we spoke with are quick to point out there really are no strict rules here—it’s about your style and what you love—there are a few key mistakes that pop up most often when people start styling with antique and vintage decor. These designers have the scoop on how to avoid those missteps and make your treasures, and your entire room, shine.

Committing to a massive piece of furniture or tasking yourself with overhauling an entire room with antique or vintage pieces can get overwhelming fast. You may make decisions you don’t love down the line.

If you decide you’re going to decorate a whole room in a limited amount of time, and you’re set on sourcing antique or vintage items, you’ll likely end up settling for things just because you need them, says Nancy Cavaliere, a New York–based interior decorator, stylist, and vintage collector. Go slow and start small.

“I’d rather live with an empty corner while I wait for the perfect piece than fill it with something I don’t love,” she notes. “The most interesting homes evolve over time. I think a room should tell the story of the person living in it, not the story of one shopping trip.”

Ease your way into decorating with vintage and antiques by starting small. Think one piece of art, one mirror, one vase.

“Don’t overthink it,” West advises. “Take a small piece, try it out in the room for a couple of days, see how it feels.” Do you like the piece in that spot? Does it work with other elements of the room? Does it inspire other styling choices for the space? Try moving it around and interacting with it to find its perfect positioning and see where that takes you.

As you start out getting a feel for that smaller piece, remember that just because it’s unique doesn’t mean it has to be the focal point of the whole room.

“Don’t treat vintage pieces like they have to be the star of the room simply because they’re old,” says Cavaliere. “Not every antique needs a pedestal. Sometimes the most beautiful thing you can do is let an extraordinary piece sit comfortably alongside something completely ordinary.”

Fully incorporating vintage or antique pieces into a space imbues the room with that richness and intrigue.

“These pieces give feelings to a room,” West says. “If you put something in a corner, as you transition throughout the room, it gives this element of surprise.”

This includes creating high-low mixes, says Cavaliere, who points out, “a $20 flea market find can look incredible next to an expensive designer piece. It’s all about how you put things together.”

In fact, that mix is something to strive for, says Alex Bass, founder of Salon 21, Art Advisory & Interior Design Firm.

“I love a contrast, like a room with modern architecture that leans heavily into antiques or vice versa. If you want to incorporate pieces from Pottery Barn, CB2, that’s fine, but there are also so many affordable vintage finds. And something like an antique light fixture can make all of these pieces work.”

Speaking of contrasts, all of our designer sources say a very common misconception they encounter is that you can only decorate a room with pieces from the same era.

“Just because you have a Victorian chair doesn’t mean you need a Victorian living room,” Cavaliere says. “My own home is a mix of different periods, styles, colors, and patterns, and that's what gives it personality. I love the tension between pieces that seemingly have nothing in common. And it's fun!”

Bass cites the example of Art Deco being popular right now, while articles are claiming midcentury modern is “out.” She encourages people to think of pieces less rigidly, assuming they have to commit fully to one style, or toss another style because someone declared a new one the next big thing.

“These are not ‘trends,’ these are design periods that already existed,” she explains. “It’s about having the eye to pull pieces from different periods that speak to you and make sense in your room.” You can mix Art Deco and midcentury modern, for instance, by using elements characteristic of them both, like marble, warm woods, mixed metals, and bold color pops.

There can be a fear with vintage and antique pieces that changing anything “ruins” them. But unless it’s a significantly valuable piece you’re investing in or something you plan on having appraised, Bass says, embrace the ability to make special pieces work for your space.

“Don’t be afraid to reupholster,” she says—and not just if the fabric is in poor shape. “If I buy a piece and the structure is beautiful but I want to add a contemporary fabric and print, that can make for a fun contrast with the frame.”

For example, Bass fell in love with a modular midcentury sectional, but its fabric didn’t quite complement the space she was designing, so she chose a contemporary fabric that worked with its aesthetic.

This, Cavaliere points out, frees you up from obsessing over condition when shopping for pieces. If something has great bones but its fabric or finish has seen better days—and if you love it—don’t pass it up. Remember you can reupholster, strip and stain or paint, and repair.

Just factor the cost of those fixes into the price of the item to help make your decision. Especially because, as Bass advises, it’s better to consult a professional unless you’re well-versed in certain projects. Stripping and staining a wood piece is fairly easy, but when it comes to reupholstering and other repairs, you’ll likely actually save money by outsourcing to a pro rather than potentially damaging the piece yourself.

Similarly, it’s typical for people to feel they can’t actually make use of vintage and antique pieces, because they think they’re too precious. But especially considering how well-made many older pieces are, they’re begging to be put to work. Again, it’s actually incorporating them into your everyday life that gives your room and entire home that sense of personalization, curation, and storytelling.

“One hundred years ago, people were using their silver,” West says. “In my kitchen, you’re eating with hotel silver.”

This includes getting creative with how you use antique and vintage pieces—don’t feel bound by what something is “supposed to” be for.

“One of my favorite tricks is turning vintage drapes and valances into bed canopies,” Cavaliere says. “I did this in my daughter’s room and I absolutely love it.” Cavaliere also finds unexpected uses for small vessels and decorative objects: a creamer and sugar set in the bathroom to hold Q-tips and cotton balls; dishes, trays, and crystal bowls to sort accessories in her closet.

“A vintage china cabinet doesn't have to live in a dining room just because it was designed to hold dishes,” she says. “One of my favorite tricks is using china cabinets to display and store shoes and handbags in a dressing room or closet. Why spend a ton of money on custom cabinetry when you can repurpose a beautiful vintage piece that has so much more character?”

West agrees. “Purchase an armoire, flip the doors, add mirrors, and make it a bar,” West suggests. “Find an old door knocker you love? Hang a towel off of it. Use a tablecloth to make a pillow. Tell a story.”

#6826. Hublot Just Relaunched Its First Watch. Founder Carlo Crocco Explains How It Changed Everything.

In Milan, celebrating the release of the Classic Fusion Tributo, I got to catch up with the guy who altered the aesthetics of modern watchmaking.

If you spend a lot of time following the world of watches, Hublot’s origin story might feel as familiar as one of the fairytales you heard at bedtime as a child. If you don’t, though, it might feel just as fantastical as some yarn about a princess who feels a pea under a stack of mattresses or a prince who slays a fire-breathing dragon.

You start with an unlikely hero. This time around, it’s an Italian entrepreneur with a big, bold, brazen idea. He wants to make a watch, something unlike anyone has ever seen. It’ll be gold, with an angular case shaped like the porthole on a ship. Not so crazy yet. But wait. Because, breaking entirely with the conventions of the day, the strap won’t be made of exotic leather or precious metal but rubber. Humble, industrial rubber.

This is exactly what Hublot founder Carlo Crocco envisioned nearly half a century ago, and what he brought to the Basel Watch Fair—pretty much the place to be if you wanted to be a part of the business of timekeeping—in 1980. He spent three years developing the idea, all without doing a bit of market research. He just believed in his vision. And then the fair began.

“I was very anxious, because for the first two days, no one came into our booth,” he says. But the anxiety didn’t have long to fester. Word was spreading. And then? People galore. “On the third day, we realized that everyone was speaking about us because it was really something different from all the other watches.”

That first watch—36mm in diameter, with a quartz movement and a black rubber strap—quickly became a hit. Industry buzz gave way to the kind of clientele most brands can only imagine. The King of Greece bought one in Gstaad for himself and another for his brother-in-law, the King of Spain. More royals followed. (“By the way, they’re all related,” Crocco quips.)

But the biggest thing wasn’t getting insiders to a booth at a trade fair or convincing kings to strap Hublot watches—named, if you’re curious, for the French word for porthole—on their wrists. It was changing how the industry thought about the way watches are made.

“At that time, the mentality of watchmaking was very classical,” Crocco says. “They couldn’t think, normal people, of putting together rubber and gold. I personally thought it was a really good idea because it was against the current. It was very risky, but if you don’t risk, you have no result.”

The result was a watch industry that completely recalibrated itself. Crocco says that within four or five years of the original Hublot’s release, the brand was already dealing with imitators. Now, the idea of combining disparate materials is firmly embedded in the minds of both consumers and designers. You can see it on watches from brands that sell at the local department store and brands that only sell to folks who haven’t seen the inside of one of those since the first time their private jet took off. Crocco’s big idea changed the whole game.

Which means it’s a pretty good time to go back to the beginning of this thing. Last month in Milan, Hublot debuted a new take on that first, groundbreaking watch. Dubbed the Hublot Classic Fusion Tributo, it’s an homage but not a nuts-and-bolts recreation. Instead of the original 36mm case, it’s been upsized to a more 2026-appropriate 38mm. And instead of a quartz movement—Crocco thought it was “more practical” at the time but has come around to the enduring appeal of mechanical construction—a self-winding HUB1711 movement with a 50-hour power reserve keeps things ticking along. Oh, and now you can get it in white gold, too.

Then there’s that rubber strap. When Crocco designed the original Hublot, he was insistent that the strap was entirely straight; he felt it was crucial to the aesthetic proposition. On the Tributo, it’s ever-so-slightly, almost imperceptibly tapered, a necessary concession to keep the proportions intact after increasing the case size (which, handily, lets that automatic movement sit comfortably inside).

But, as with the first Hublot, it’s not all about tech specs and watch-nerd particulars. It’s about the spirit of the thing. And the spirit of this watch stays true to that pioneering design. There are twelve screws around the bezel, so you can keep an eye on the hour despite the dial’s lack of indices—something Crocco says was a non-negotiable when he started consulting on the new release. The yellow gold version keeps the abyss-like black lacquer dial and the white gold throws us a tiny (and welcome) curveball by using onyx. The combo of precious metal and rubber still hits, 46 years later.

If you prod him how he feels about the way that he shifted the watch industry, Crocco won’t brag. He left Hublot nearly 20 years ago, when it sold to LVMH. He knows his idea changed things; he’s happy about it. But this isn’t his day job anymore. Still, when he’s asked if he could be considered the godfather of fusing high and low in watchmaking—and you give him a minute to ponder it—he’ll tell you the truth.

#6825. The Women Who “Stopped the Wedding”—and Never Looked Back

“Ihad already bought my wedding dress, asked my bridesmaids, and started imagining what was supposed to be one of the happiest days of my life,” 27-year-old Sacha Smith tells me. “I just didn’t know that the relationship I believed I was in wasn’t the reality.”

After discovering her fiancé had been cheating on her for the entirety of their engagement, Smith called off the wedding. She is one of hundreds of women who—as part of a viral TikTok trend—shared their story of coming to their senses and ending their relationship right before a wedding.

In these TikTok videos, women share photos and videos of themselves in their previous relationships—sometimes even trying their wedding dresses on—with the words of Ashe’s viral song “Stop the Wedding!” playing in the background. In the song, Ashe sings, “Even though the table’s set, the guests are waiting, you can burn the dress and run away. Stop the wedding.” The song is catchy, but it’s also a warning cry to women that it’s never too late to save yourself from a lifetime of misery tied to the wrong person.

And it often actually takes something as monumental as an impending wedding to figure out a relationship isn’t working. “When you’ve spent years overriding your own feelings to maintain relationships, it can become really difficult to recognize what you actually want,” Cheryl Groskopf, LMFT, tells Cosmopolitan. “A wedding makes everything feel real and tangible because now you’re facing the possibility of committing to this dynamic for the rest of your life. And sometimes that forces you to confront something you’ve been trying really hard to avoid.”

This was the case for Katherine Woller, 39. There was no cheating partner or big fallout—just a gut feeling she couldn’t ignore. The feeling started the moment her boyfriend got down on one knee. Even with the gorgeous backdrop of Champagne, France, she couldn’t shake the thought that she was making a mistake by saying yes.

It was only two weeks before her wedding when Woller stood in front of the mirror wearing her would-be wedding dress during a fitting, feeling utterly detached. “I felt completely out of control and lost, as if I were watching someone else’s life,” she says. “At that moment, I couldn’t ignore my inner voice any longer and knew it was time to make a big, hard choice.”

Despite the magnitude of an “I shouldn’t be doing this” feeling, a wedding can make it seem impossible to walk away. This is called a sunk cost fallacy. And even though you understand (via its label) that it isn’t true, in the moment, it feels like you’ve put too much in to the relationship to leave it. Therapist Justena Kavanagh, LCSW, explains that once you’re engaged, the deposits, the guest list, and other people’s expectations build momentum. “Stopping can feel harder than going through with it.”

Tishayla Williams was 30 when she was engaged. She had bought the dress, finalized the venue, and was the only woman in her family who wasn’t married or a mother. The pressure was very real.

But when she had been followed out of a cafe by a strange man and called her fiancé crying, his first response had been, “What were you wearing?”

“I told him to come collect his two-carat engagement ring,” she says.

When your gut is screaming at you that you’re with the wrong person, ignoring it feels like abandoning yourself. This is why so many women stop the wedding—and why Ashe wants to remind us that despite our fears of letting others down, it’s always better to walk away.

“I realized that the money spent and the embarrassment of canceling a wedding were nothing compared to the cost of marrying someone who had betrayed my trust,” Smith says. “I’d much rather have a wedding dress I never wore than enter a marriage that was false.”

Women who call off a wedding usually think that facing their family and friends will be the most difficult part. But Smith, Woller, and Williams all agree that their loved ones met them with grace and understanding. Woller even says that confiding in her friends and family led to closer connections and more vulnerability.

“I think the hardest part was accepting that the future I’d imagined wasn’t going to happen,” Smith says. “I think people underestimate how much emotion goes into that. You picture the day, the people who will be there, the life you’ll build afterward, and even the family you might have together.”

And despite knowing that the relationship is wrong or that calling the wedding off is the right decision, it doesn’t make ending the relationship any easier. “Even when you’re certain, you’re still human,” Williams says. “I spent the first few days sad, wondering if I’d walked away from my one chance at a fairy tale.”

In the part of the audio that doesn’t usually make it to the TikTok videos, Ashe sings, “This isn’t cold feet, these aren’t fireworks. The sky is menacing and that’s an iceberg.”

But nerves and wedding day jitters are always advertised to us as a normal part of your big day. How do you tell when it’s something more?

“Cold feet is usually about the commitment itself: the finality, the change in identity, the idea of ‘forever,’” Kavanagh explains. “A bad gut feeling is about the person: how they treat you, whether you feel safe and respected, and patterns you’ve been explaining away.”

Psychologist Karen Stewart, PsyD, explains that anxiety alone isn’t typically proof that something is wrong in the relationship. Instead, if you feel like you can’t trust them, don’t feel emotionally safe with them, or have unresolved patterns of conflict, these are all typically signs that the relationship isn’t sturdy enough to support a marriage. She also emphasizes that foundational issues in a relationship will not magically improve after a wedding. In fact, they’ll typically only get worse.

Some people are just anxious, and it can be hard to trust their gut feelings. But when you analyze where your anxiety is coming from, you can typically pinpoint whether it’s just your body reacting to a huge life choice or something deeper about your relationship.

“Sometimes we know far more than we are willing to admit,” Woller says.

It always comes back to thinking about the connection and compatibility you have with your partner. “If you can picture yourself five years from now, crying in the bathroom at 3 a.m., wondering why the hell you married this person, there’s your answer,” Williams says.

Calling a wedding off can be devastating. You’ve often spent months (or even years) planning a day, telling everyone you know, and picturing a future with this person that you love. Losing all of that at once can feel like something you’ll never get over.

But this is why Ashe sings over and over again that none of it matters. Everything is secondary to your happiness and emotional safety. And as you can see through the countless TikTok videos, deciding to call off a wedding can save your life.

“I genuinely believe it was both the worst and best thing that’s ever happened to me,” Smith says. Since calling off her wedding, she’s traveled extensively, made strides in her career, and learned more about herself than ever before.

Williams feels the same: “I’ve spent the years since investing in myself and my career, and I’ve learned that life doesn’t begin with a proposal or end with a canceled wedding.”

Although Woller regrets how she handled certain things in that season of her life—and is even sorry for the pain she caused her former partner—she’s learned to move on from the guilt and shame she felt in the aftermath of breaking her engagement.

“I still feel 100 percent confident that it was the right decision for both of us, and I am thankful every day that I dared to make such a life-altering choice.”

#6821. An Expert Guide to Nail Health, According to Professionals

Here’s exactly how to keep them strong and healthy

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

As manicure trends evolve with new lacquers, changing shapes, and viral techniques, beauty lovers often compromise nail health for aesthetics. While many nail formulas have improved over the years to prioritize strength, uniformity, and finish, experts say incremental habits—from lifestyle shifts to resisting the urge to peel off chipped polish—can ultimately play the biggest role in keeping your nails in excellent condition.

“The future of manicures isn’t just about the latest color trends or how long your gel polish lasts, but how well we care for the nails underneath,” Krisztina van der Boom, founder of DryBy, previously told Harper’s Bazaar. Hand, foot, and nail expert Margaret Dabbs agrees, urging nail enthusiasts to “Adopt a dedicated nail care routine and stick to it.” But what does an effective routine actually look like? Along with keeping your nails clean and hydrated with an emollient moisturizer, like Caudalíe’s Vinotherapist Hand and Nail Cream, professionals suggest keeping a cuticle oil, such as Dior’s Huile Abricot, in your bag for quick touch-ups.

“Cuticle oil is the one product I would never skip,” says Peach Bling founder Sandra Wei. “Look for a formula with jojoba oil, which has small enough molecules to absorb into the nail and surrounding skin.” Wei encourages her clients to apply it daily, ideally before bed, and to treat it as a small ritual. “A rich hand cream and gloves for dishwashing and cleaning also make a real difference,” continues Wei, “since water and harsh detergents are among the most common causes of dry, weak nails.” Maintaining nail and cuticle hydration is also key for mature nails, as they tend to grow more slowly and hold less moisture as we age, which can contribute to brittleness and vertical ridges.

Soft Services Theraplush Overnight Repair Treatment

Dior Huile Abricot Nutritive Serum for Nails and Cuticles

Professionals have debated whether it’s safe to trim your cuticles, since cuticles guard nail beds against bacteria and infection; that's why they often advise pushing them back instead of removing them entirely. “As long as you're not cutting any live skin and damaging your cuticles, gently pushing them back and buffing away excess skin will actually help improve the health and the appearance of your cuticles and will help make your manicure last as long as possible while looking salon-quality,” says Olivia Van Iderstine, vice president of education and manicure trend expert at Olive & June. Prep your fingertips with cuticle oil or a soak in warm, soapy water before a manicure, then use a disposable orangewood stick or stainless steel pusher, such as JinSoon’s HyperCare Cuticle Pusher + Reducer, to improve polish cohesion and create an elongated nail look.

Consistent, gentle filing can also keep nails strong, regardless of your shape preference, because a smooth surface guards against unwanted splits, rips, and tears that can cause long-term damage. While there’s nothing wrong with a budget-friendly emery board, professionals say investing in the right tools provides the right resistance without compromising your nails' integrity. Dabbs is a fan of crystal nail files because they can help prevent micro-splits that lead to peeling and breakage, though the best results depend on not overfiling.

And if you usually opt for gel polish manicures, they don’t have to be a deal breaker for nail health. Several options, from builder gel to Japanese gel and more, provide longevity, durability, and style without weakening natural nails.

“Japanese gel systems are known for their precision and for their focus on preserving the natural nail,” continues Wei. “The prep is minimal and gentle, so we avoid heavy buffing of the nail plate. The gel itself is applied in fine, structured layers that are flexible and sit close to the natural nail.” Peach Bling prioritizes formulas that are free of HEMA and TPO, two ingredients commonly linked to gel allergies and sensitivities. “The result is a manicure that looks refined, wears beautifully, and treats the nail underneath with care,” explains the founder. Bazaar editors love builder gel, as it works like Japanese gel, supporting natural nails with a rounded apex to reinforce their sturdiness.

Experts advise keeping a gel set on for no longer than four weeks, as regrowth changes the nail's balance, ultimately causing unwanted stress and making breaks more likely. Once gel polish begins to lift or crack, it’s often a sign that removal is necessary—and that process can often be more damaging than simply wearing a gel formula in the first place. “What protects nail integrity is how the gel is applied and, even more importantly, how it is removed,” adds Wei. “With careful, gentle removal, there is no need to take a ‘break’ between appointments.”

For more than 150 years, Harper’s Bazaar has been the preeminent fashion and beauty resource for women at every age. We cover what’s new and what’s next in beauty by working with the world’s leading authorities in dermatology, plastic surgery, cosmetics, skincare, hair care, and fragrance. Every story we publish has been thoroughly researched and vetted by our team of beauty editors and industry experts.

But his campaign for governor was something of a roller coaster.
